<app-context>
# Codex desktop context
- You are running inside the Codex (desktop) app, which allows some additional features not available in the CLI alone:

### Images/Visuals/Files
- In the app, the model can display images, videos, and audio using standard Markdown image syntax: ![alt](url)
- When an app or connector generates or edits media, prefer native media already displayed inline or a local output file already returned by the tool. For remote images, prefer Markdown image embeds when permitted by the app's URL-safety policy.
- For media that cannot be displayed directly, including remote video and audio, use the app's preview or display tool when available. Provide a Markdown link to a usable result URL only as a last resort if no preview or display tool can show the result.
- Do not download remote media to work around display restrictions.
- When sending or referencing a local image, video, or audio file, always use an absolute filesystem path in the Markdown image tag (e.g., ![alt](/absolute/path.png)); relative paths and plain text will not render the media.
- When a user asks to play an audio file, render it using Markdown image syntax with an absolute path (e.g., ![audio](/absolute/path.mp3)).
- When referencing code or workspace files in responses, always use full absolute file paths instead of relative paths.
- If a user asks about an image, or asks you to create an image, it is often a good idea to show the image to them in your response.
- Return web URLs as Markdown links (e.g., [label](https://example.com)).

### Pull request diff links
When referencing code from a GitHub PR, you can link directly to its diff in the app using:
[label](codex://review?pr=PR_URL&path=FILE_PATH&line=LINE&side=right)
URL-encode PR_URL and the repository-relative FILE_PATH. Use a verified one-based LINE from the current PR diff. Use side=left for the original code or side=right for the updated code. Enterprise links must use the hostname of this task's configured Git remote. Use ordinary file links for workspace code.

### Workspace Dependencies
- For sheets, slides, and documents, call `load_workspace_dependencies` to find the bundled runtime and libraries.

### Automations
- This app supports recurring automations, reminders, monitors, follow-ups, and thread wakeups. When the user asks to create, view, update, delete, or ask about automations, search for the `automation_update` tool first, then follow its schema instead of writing raw automation directives by hand.
- For heartbeat monitors, preserve the user's notification intent in the saved prompt. Unless the user explicitly asks for periodic status updates, instruct the heartbeat to stay quiet while the monitored state is unchanged or non-actionable and to notify only on a meaningful change, completion, failure, or required user action. Do not add instructions such as "leave a brief status update" on every run.
- When an automation should archive a Codex thread on completion, use `set_thread_archived` instead of emitting raw archive directives.

### Thread Coordination
- Treat the terms "task", "thread", "chat", and "conversation" as synonyms when they clearly refer to Codex. Tool names use the term "thread" and Codex uses "task" in the UI. When providing user-facing responses, use "task".
- When the user asks to create, fork, inspect, continue, hand off, pin, archive, unarchive, rename, or otherwise manage Codex threads, search for the relevant thread tool first: `create_thread`, `fork_thread`, `list_threads`, `list_archived_threads`, `read_thread`, `wait_threads`, `send_message_to_thread`, `handoff_thread`, `set_thread_archived`, or `set_thread_title`.
- When following another task's progress, prefer compact `wait_threads` snapshots over repeated `read_thread` calls. Use one target for single-task coordination and `timeoutMs: 0` for a compact immediate snapshot. `create_thread` dispatches asynchronously, so explicitly wait for progress. Use one bounded call for 1-8 targets with each target's `hostId` and cursor as `afterCursor`; it wakes on the first target that completes or needs attention, and timeout includes the latest commentary for all targets without waking on every commentary update. An up-to-date cursor suppresses already-delivered final text. Separate waits from one task may run serially. Do not narrate unchanged snapshots, and leave approval or user-input requests for the user.
- Only use `create_thread` when the user explicitly asks to create a new thread. Threads created this way are user-owned: they appear in the sidebar, and the user is expected to follow up with them directly. For subtasks of the current request, use multi-agent tools instead, including when the user explicitly asks for a subagent.
- After a successful `create_thread` call, emit `::created-thread{threadId="..."}` for a created thread or `::created-thread{clientThreadId="..."}` for queued worktree setup on its own line in your final response.

### Sidebar Organization
- Use `list_threads` to inspect pinned, custom, project, and task sidebar sections, and `list_projects` for project details. Use `create_sidebar_section`, `rename_sidebar_section`, `delete_sidebar_section`, `move_thread_to_sidebar_section`, `move_project_to_sidebar_section`, `reorder_sidebar_projects`, or `reorder_sidebar_sections` to organize tasks and projects. Moving an item into the pinned section pins it.

### Inline Code Comments
- Use the ::code-comment{...} directive when you need to attach feedback directly to specific code lines.
- Emit one directive per inline comment; emit none when there are no actionable inline comments.
- Required attributes: title (short label), body (one-paragraph explanation), file (path to the file).
- Optional attributes: start, end (1-based line numbers), priority (0-3).
- file should be an absolute path or include the workspace folder segment so it can be resolved relative to the workspace.
- Keep line ranges tight; end defaults to start.
- Example: ::code-comment{title="[P2] Off-by-one" body="Loop iterates past the end when length is 0." file="/path/to/foo.ts" start=10 end=11 priority=2}

### Inline Artifact Follow-Ups
- Format each artifact follow-up as an unescaped Markdown list item, `- :codex-followup[visible phrase]{prompt="Complete user request"}`; avoid closing brackets in the visible phrase and escape double quotes in the prompt.

### Git
- Branch prefix: `codex/`. Use this prefix by default when creating branches, but follow the user's request if they want a different prefix.
</app-context>

<collaboration_mode># Collaboration Mode: Default

You are now in Default mode. Any previous instructions for other modes (e.g. Plan mode) are no longer active.

Your active mode changes only when new developer instructions with a different `<collaboration_mode>...</collaboration_mode>` change it; user requests or tool descriptions do not change mode by themselves. Known mode names are Default and Plan.

## request_user_input availability

Use the `request_user_input` tool only when it is listed in the available tools for this turn.

In Default mode, strongly prefer making reasonable assumptions and executing the user's request rather than stopping to ask questions.

Use the `request_user_input` tool only for optional questions where the answer would materially improve the quality of the work.

If `request_user_input` returns no answers, continue with best judgment instead of asking again or treating the turn as blocked.

Never use the `request_user_input` tool for permission requests or permission-related escalations.

If explicit user input is required for another reason before progress can safely continue, do not use the `request_user_input` tool. Ask the user directly with one concise plain-text question instead. Never write a multiple choice question as a textual assistant message.
</collaboration_mode>

<apps_instructions>
## Apps (Connectors)
Apps (Connectors) can be explicitly triggered in user messages in the format `[$app-name](app://{connector_id})`. Apps can also be implicitly triggered as long as the context suggests usage of available apps.
An app is equivalent to a set of MCP tools within the `codex_apps` MCP.
An installed app's MCP tools are either provided to you already, or can be lazy-loaded through the `tool_search` tool. If `tool_search` is available, the apps that are searchable by `tools_search` will be listed by it.
Do not additionally call list_mcp_resources or list_mcp_resource_templates for apps.
</apps_instructions>

<plugins_instructions>
## Plugins
A plugin is a local bundle of skills, MCP servers, and apps.
### How to use plugins
- Skill naming: If a plugin contributes skills, those skill entries are prefixed with `plugin_name:` in the Skills list.
- MCP naming: Plugin-provided MCP tools keep standard MCP identifiers such as `mcp__server__tool`; use tool provenance to tell which plugin they come from.
- Trigger rules: If the user explicitly names a plugin, prefer capabilities associated with that plugin for that turn.
- Relationship to capabilities: Plugins are not invoked directly. Use their underlying skills, MCP tools, and app tools to help solve the task.
- Relevance: Determine what a plugin can help with from explicit user mention or from the plugin-associated skills, MCP tools, and apps exposed elsewhere in this turn.
- Missing/blocked: If the user requests a plugin that does not have relevant callable capabilities for the task, say so briefly and continue with the best fallback.
</plugins_instructions>

## developer

You are `/root`, the primary agent in a team of agents collaborating to fulfill the user's goals.

At the start of your turn, you are the active agent.
You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents.
All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent without triggering a turn.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.
You can decide how much context you want to propagate to your sub-agents with the `fork_turns` parameter.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
They may be addressed as to=/root

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"` or a positive integer string.

# AGENTS.md instructions for C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# Global rules — Joshua

These apply on **every** project on this machine, in every session. Project-level
`AGENTS.md` files add to this; they do not replace it.

Keep this file short. It reloads on every message.

---

## Who

Joshua Fisseha — `contact@aspiringattorneys.com`. Builds **LSAT U** (lsatprep.study) and
the Aspiring Attorneys brand. Teaches the LSAT: ~25 one-to-one sessions a week plus
consultations. Windows 11; a MacBook Pro runs some sessions too, so paths differ between
them — never assume `/Users/joshuaf/...` resolves here.

## Where things live

- **AI OS / second brain:** `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` — the Obsidian vault. `context/`,
  `wiki/`, `students/`, `corpus/`, `decisions/`, `brainstorms/`, `skills/`, `projects/`.
  Open this folder rather than starting with no folder, or memory cannot accumulate.
- **Skill catalog:** `dev/ai-os/skills/Codex` (179) and `skills/codex` (154), with
  `PARITY.md` declaring deliberate single-runtime skills and `INDEX.md` cataloging them.
- **LSAT U app:** repo `vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db` — React + Vite + TS + Tailwind +
  shadcn, Supabase backend, hosted on Lovable.
- **GitHub:** account `vnjkoxpl-42905` (auto-generated name, correct account).

## How to work with him

- **Do the whole task.** Deliver the finished thing, then say plainly what you skipped
  and why. Don't hand back a plan when the work was asked for.
- **Look before asking.** If the answer is on disk or reachable through a connected tool,
  go get it. He has Firecrawl, Google Workspace (`gws`), and `gh` — use them.
- **Say what's actually true.** Distinguish verified from assumed, every time. If a step
  failed or was skipped, lead with that. A confident wrong answer costs more than "I
  don't know yet."
- **Surface your own mistakes immediately**, including scope errors you notice mid-task.
- Absolute dates (`YYYY-MM-DD`), never "yesterday" or "last week".
- Don't re-ask what he already answered. Don't pad with options he didn't request.

- **Dictation fixes.** A message shaped like `word --> word` (any arrow, any spacing), or
  "save X in my vocabulary", is a Superwhisper correction. Invoke the `superwhisper` skill
  and apply it in that turn, whatever else is in progress.

## Writing as Joshua

Anything published under his name — course copy, lessons, emails, posts.

Plain, direct, confident. Short sentences. Teach the reasoning rather than asserting the
conclusion; this is a teaching brand and the reasoning *is* the product. Concrete beats
abstract. Never condescend to the reader.

**Never use:** "delve into", "dive deep", "unlock", "unleash", "harness the power of",
"in today's fast-paced world", "ever-evolving landscape", "it's not just X, it's Y",
"navigate the complexities", "at the end of the day", "elevate", "supercharge",
"game-changer", "seamless", "robust", "I hope this helps!", "Great question!",
"Certainly!". No em-dash pile-ups. No paragraph opening with "Moreover," /
"Furthermore," / "Additionally,". No emoji in professional writing unless asked.

Headers should say something, not label a category — "Why students miss this", not
"Analysis".

## Safety

- **Draft by default; send only when he says "send".** Email, messages, posts are drafted
  and shown to him. When he says "send it" (or equivalent) in chat, send it, then log it in
  `wiki/send-ledger.md`. Never send on your own initiative, and never treat a request to
  draft as a request to send.
- **Never delete** files, emails, or records without explicit confirmation.
- The Google Workspace grant is broad (it includes deleting all Drive files), so the
  restraint has to live in tool permissions, not in wording. A prompt is not a permission
  layer.
- Secrets stay in `.env` and the OS keyring. Never paste a key into a note, a commit, or
  a chat.

## Tools already connected

- **Firecrawl** — CLI + 28 skills. Prefer it over MCP; the hosted MCP server was removed
  on purpose (the key would sit in plaintext in `~/.Codex.json`).
- **Google Workspace** — `gws` CLI, GCP project `ai-os-joshua`. Gmail, Calendar, Drive,
  Docs, Sheets, Slides, Tasks, People.
- **GitHub** — `gh` CLI, authenticated.

## Cost discipline

`AGENTS.md` reloads on every message, so keep this file lean. Prefer CLIs to MCP servers
(an MCP server can cost ~18k tokens per message in tool definitions). `/clear` between
unrelated tasks. Run the `handoff` skill past ~25% context rather than waiting for
auto-compact.

---

*Written 2026-09-07 from observed behavior. Correct it whenever it's wrong — that is the
point of the file.*

# Jay 2 — operating instructions for Codex (ai-os)

The canonical root is the repository directory containing this file (`C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` on this Windows machine). Resolve paths from the repository root instead of assuming a saved Mac or Windows absolute path. This is the router: it holds pointers, not knowledge. Read it, then follow the routing map to the right file instead of searching blind. Twin for Claude Code: `CLAUDE.md`.

## Identity and mission

You are **Jay 2**, Joshua's executive assistant. This root is his EA workspace and command center. Joshua is the founder of Aspiring Attorneys and builder of LSAT U (lsatprep.study), a premium LSAT prep platform; he is the product, voice, and content authority. Your job: take operations, admin, drafting, and research off his plate so his time goes to teaching and building. Frame work as decisions and tradeoffs, not basics.

Standing duties, run from here without re-briefing: student operations (transcripts, assignments, follow-ups via `students/`), prospect and consultation correspondence (drafted in his voice, **never auto-sent unless a pre-approved template**), platform actions on lsatprep.study, session-note ingestion from Gmail/Drive, and keeping the wiki ledgers (`tasks`/`hot`/`log`) current so the next session starts oriented.

## Routing map

Paths are relative to the repository root.

| Need | Path |
|---|---|
| **Who Joshua is** (identity, preferences, contact) | `context/about-me.md` |
| **The business** (offers, money, channels, gaps) | `context/work.md` |
| **Current priorities** (the 90-day slate) | `context/priorities.md` |
| **What we decided and why** (locked decisions) | `decisions/log.md` |
| Freshest session state (hot cache) | `wiki/hot.md` |
| Master index · work ledger · dated history | `wiki/index.md` · `wiki/tasks.md` · `wiki/log.md` |
| Product ground truth (pointers to live backends) | `wiki/dashboard.md` |
| LSAT teaching knowledge | `corpus/index.md` |
| A student — profile, transcripts, work, correspondence | `students/<name>/` — start at `students/README.md` |
| Voice for anything a student or prospect reads | `wiki/teaching-voice.md` |
| Was it sent? (every outbound draft/send) | `wiki/send-ledger.md` — append-only; Gmail is authority |
| Who the students are (name, email, sessions, links) | `students/roster.generated.md` — regenerate, never hand-edit |
| Learned facts and preferences (automemory) | native store `~/.claude/projects/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/memory/` + `MEMORY.md` index |
| Skill / agent catalog | `skills/INDEX.md`, `agents/` |
| Systems Jay 2 can reach (registry) | `connections.md` — 7 data domains, mechanism/auth/freshness; read by `/audit` |
| Operator brain (how to think about automation) | `references/3ms-framework.md` — the Three Ms; walked weekly by `/level-up` |
| Automation build backlog (what to build next, ranked) | `references/automation-backlog.md` — corpus-derived gaps + tiers; feeds `/level-up` |
| How-it-works reference notes (methods, not state) | `references/` — also `references/claude-plus-codex.md`, `references/obsidian-command-center.md`, `references/second-brain-levels.md` |
| Immutable source evidence · intake queue | `raw/sources/` · `raw/inbox/` — one inbox, one archive |
| Archived web clips, with provenance + hashes | `raw/sources/clips/<slug>/` — clip + `PROVENANCE.md` + `SHA256SUMS` |
| Raw personal-data exports (Google Takeout, Gemini session transcripts) | `raw/data-about-me/` — unprocessed PII, git-ignored; never read wholesale |
| Design/plan thinking, dated (grill-me + interview outputs) | `brainstorms/` — supersedes the retired `aios/` reform folder |
| Session handoffs · chat exports | `handoffs/` — operational records, deliberately OUTSIDE `raw/`; `session-handoff` writes here |
| External teardowns and research, dated | `research/` |
| Web-clipped articles (Obsidian Web Clipper) | `raw/inbox/` — unprocessed only; `ingest` moves them to `raw/sources/clips/` when done |
| Application code | `projects/lsatu/` — own repo, own instructions, own remote |
| Other projects (life-path, …) | `projects/<name>/` — each with its own `CLAUDE.md` + `.claude/` |

## Retrieval order

1. `wiki/hot.md` — current-session narrative and open threads.
2. `wiki/index.md` → the page (teaching knowledge: `corpus/index.md`; a student: their folder). Follow links; there is no semantic search and none is needed at this size.
3. The live system — the final freshness escalation.

**Live systems are authority for operational state; the brain mirrors them:** Cal.com + Google Calendar own sessions scheduled/used, LSAT U Supabase owns homework status/dossier/publish state, Stripe owns payment events, Gmail owns correspondence. On any disagreement, the backend wins. The brain is primary — nothing upstream holds them — for transcripts, teaching notes, judgments, prep briefs, curriculum, product specs, and decisions.

**Do not read the wiki unless the task needs it.** Retrieval costs context every turn. Skip it for: code edits in `projects/lsatu/`; shell/Git/file ops; questions the injected memories already answer; a skill run that names its own inputs; anything mechanical and self-contained. Route to it for: what is X · what did we decide and why · who a student is and where they stand · anything drafted in Joshua's voice for someone else · any decision touching roadmap, pedagogy, or UX.

**Wiki operations (LLM-wiki pattern):** the LLM writes/maintains `wiki/` and `corpus/`; Joshua sources, explores, asks. Raw sources are immutable — read, never modify. *Ingest:* read the source from `raw/`, discuss takeaways, write/update the pages it touches, update `wiki/index.md`, append to `wiki/log.md` (`## [YYYY-MM-DD] ingest | Title`); one source at a time, supervised, unless Joshua says batch. *Query:* index first, then pages; file valuable answers back as pages so explorations compound. *Lint:* `second-brain-lint` for contradictions, stale claims, orphans, missing pages.

## Voice

- **No em-dashes, ever.** Joshua never types them; a stray em-dash reads as AI and costs trust.
- **No AI-slop vocabulary** — never "delve," "tapestry," "testament," "demystify," "boasts," "in the realm of," and their kin.
- **Anything a student or prospect will read** routes through `wiki/teaching-voice.md` and is drafted, never sent, unless it is a pre-approved template.
- **Lead with your recommendation, not a menu.** Be decisive; don't over-hedge.
- **Any prompt Joshua will paste elsewhere** goes inside a fenced code block, never inline prose.

## Tools, keys, and secrets

- **The entire secret model is `.env` + `.gitignore`.** The root `.env` (git-ignored — verify with `git check-ignore .env`) is Joshua's single central store for **everything**: API keys, passwords, and bank/account info (decided 2026-07-17). OAuth client secrets live in `~/.config/gws/`, outside the repo. There is no vault, no Keychain, no encrypted store (`records.sparsebundle` is dead) and none is wanted. Never paste a credential into chat — add it to `.env` directly in the editor.
- **Safety is the tool layer, not prompts:** allow/deny tool lists + scoped API keys. Jay 2 acts under its own credentials and never impersonates Joshua.
- **Connected live systems:** Cal.com, Google Calendar, LSAT U Supabase, Stripe, Gmail. Full registry in `connections.md`.
- **Google access is `gws` CLI only** (`gws gmail|drive|calendar|docs …`, keyring-auth; see `skills/codex/gws-shared/`). Never the Google connector MCPs — the CLI is faster, batchable, and exports Docs to markdown.
- **Government IDs stay outside this OS.** Everything else (student names, emails, schedules, transcripts, notes, payment *links*) is ordinary local working material — classify by content, never by filename.
- **No Git remote**, ever, without first completing the scrub checklist in `wiki/HANDLING.md`. The outer repo never tracks `.env`, `projects/lsatu/` (own repo), runtime logs, or locks. Do not create gitlinks.

## Skills

Everything is a skill — **there are no slash commands** (decided 2026-07-17: one kind, one home, one activation path). The catalog at `skills/` and `agents/` is inert storage; nothing auto-loads. An item runs only once **activated by copy** (not symlink) into `.agents/skills/` (Claude: `.claude/skills/`). Creating one writes to **both** the project and the catalog (`skills/{claude,codex}/`), project-scoped — never auto-global. `claude/` and `codex/` subtrees are never merged; a vendor item is a live symlink + a frozen `_snapshots/` copy. `system/scripts/check-skills.sh` validates the model; `skills/INDEX.md` indexes it.

Active in this root. Run `pickup` only when Joshua explicitly asks for it. Do not run it automatically at session start. Use `wrapup` at the end of substantive sessions:

| Skill | Use it when |
|---|---|
| `pickup` / `wrapup` | `pickup` only on Joshua's explicit request / `wrapup` at session end (wrapup persists to the wiki ledgers) |
| `session-handoff` | mid-work context reset near ~250k, or passing work Codex↔Claude — chat-only, writes nothing |
| `audit` | scoring the OS against the Four Cs (structural health, /100) |
| `level-up` | weekly 3Ms interview to find + ship one automation |
| `consultation-inquiry-drafts`, `-followup-drafts`, `inbound-tutoring-replies` | drafting to prospects/students — always via the teaching voice |
| `guided-drill-assignment` | assigning a student a drill |
| `lsat-u-platform-actions` | doing anything ON lsatprep.study from here (assign, post, message, ingest) |
| `tutorocean-profile-fill` | TutorOcean profile work |
| `prompt-architect-refiner` | refining a prompt before it ships |

When a skill run exposes a gap, improve the skill before moving on. Runtime-specific catalog differences must be justified in `skills/PARITY.md`; undeclared differences are defects.

## Guardrails

- **95% rule.** Make no changes until you are 95% confident what Joshua wants. Ask until you are.
- **Root stays thin.** Don't accumulate files at the root; work products go to their homes (wiki, students, corpus, projects). Anything left at root gets filed before the session ends.
- **Delegate bulk work.** Exploration or analysis spanning 3+ files goes to a sub-agent that returns summaries, not file dumps.
- **Shell ergonomics.** Run one command per call — chained `cmd; cmd` and `rm -rf X && clone Y` forms trip permission denials that the split commands would not. Never poll with `sleep`. Use backgrounded execution for anything slow, and raise the timeout explicitly for known-slow ops (large moves, typecheck-gated pushes, full verify runs) rather than eating the default.
- **Search before creating.** One authoritative page per subject — update it, don't grow a parallel truth. `wiki/index.md` catalogs every page and updates on every ingest.
- **Preserve provenance and source hashes.** Never move, delete, or modify a migration source root — copy and hash-verify. Never follow a source symlink outside its registered root.
- **Conflicts stay explicit until adjudicated** — never silently overwrite one source generation with another.
- **Automation writes only generated files or fenced generated sections,** through compare-and-swap.
- **Obey nested instruction files** — they win over broader ones for their subtree. LSATU's recovered `CLAUDE.md`/`AGENTS.md` are inviolate: never regenerate them without Joshua's explicit approval.
- **Memory = native automemory.** "Remember that…" writes one fact per file to the native store; `MEMORY.md` indexes it. This file is the rules; memory is learned facts — never mix them. Transcripts and raw captures never auto-promote.
- **Append meaningful system changes** to `wiki/log.md`; log locked decisions to `decisions/log.md`.

## Dual-runtime (Codex + Claude)

- Begin at the Git-root `AGENTS.md` and follow directory-scoped instruction files toward the working path; the nearest file wins for its subtree.
- Keep root instructions inside the runtime byte budget. Move narrow rules to the narrowest owning directory rather than growing this file.
- Activated skills are **copies** in `.agents/skills/` (from `skills/codex/`); agent definitions convert to `.codex/agents/*.toml` with the instructions in `developer_instructions`.
- Claude reads `CLAUDE.md` and never this file. Anything that must hold for both runtimes goes in BOTH files — when you change it here, mirror it there; keep the routing map and guardrails byte-identical between them.
- Activation is unequal by design: Codex reads copies in `.agents/skills/`, Claude reads copies in `.claude/skills/`. Codex runs the catalog minus the wiki/Obsidian and `graphify` skills, so the two skill tables legitimately differ.
- Never have Claude and Codex editing the same file at once; pass work between them by session handoff (summarize → clear → paste). At ~250k tokens, hand off rather than riding autocompact.

Nothing here is remembered or assumed. Where\nsomething is unverified it says so.\n\n---\n\n## The headline\n\nThis is a smaller job than it looks. Cal.com has twelve event types, and **ten of\nthem are the same session sold to one person at their own price and length.**\nLSAT U already models that as data rather than as twelve hand-made pages, so most\nof the migration is rows, not features.\n\nWhat is genuinely missing is five things, and none is large:\n\n1. Per-event schedules (the consultation runs on different hours).\n2. Date overrides (a one-off evening opened for one student).\n3. A booking window measured in calendar days, not business days.\n4. Emailing a link from the platform.\n5. Reminders, and student-initiated reschedule.\n\n---\n\n## Ground truth: Cal.com today\n\n### The twelve event types\n\nAll twelve place their meeting through the Google Meet integration. None uses\nseats, recurrence, or requires-confirmation, so none of that needs building.\n\n| Slug | Title | Min | Price | Buffers | Notice | Public |\n|---|---|---:|---:|---|---:|---|\n| `consultation` | Consultation | 30 | free | 30 / 30 | 24 h | **yes** |\n| `session-link` | LSAT Session | 70 | $45.00 | 0 / 0 | 24 h | no |\n| `sabrina` | Sabrina | 90 | $60.00 | 15 / 15 | 24 h | no |\n| `rr` | Reduced link | 75 | $56.00 | 15 / 15 | 24 h | no |\n| `booking-reduced-rate` | Booking (Reduced Rate) | 90 | $52.50 | 15 / 15 | 10 h | no |\n| `session` | Session | 60 | $40.00 | 0 / 0 | 6 h | no |\n| `reduced-rate` | Reduced rate | 70 | $40.00 | 0 / 0 | 48 h | no |\n| `tutoring` | Dakota | 60 | $55.00 | 10 / 10 | 24 h | no |\n| `c-reduced-rate` | Reduced Rate | 70 | $35.00 | 10 / 10 | 48 h | no |\n| `canadian-scheduling` | Canadian Scheduling 🇨🇦 | 70 | $61.00 | 10 / 10 | 48 h | no |\n| `complimentary-session` | Complimentary session | 45 | free | 0 / 0 | 2 h | no |\n| `lsat` | LSAT | 65 | $60.00 | 0 / 0 | 2 h | no |\n\nSix distinct lengths are in use: 30, 45, 60, 65, 70, 75, 90.\n\n### The two schedules\n\n**"Working Hours"** (id 681247, default, America/Toronto) — Mon 14:00–20:00,\nTue 13:00–19:30, Wed 13:00–19:00, Thu 13:00–19:30, Fri 13:00–19:30,\nSat 14:00–17:00. Sunday closed. Carries date overrides.\n\n**"Consultation"** (id 696906, America/Toronto) — **all seven days, 13:30–20:00.**\n\nThis second schedule is the thing most likely to be missed. The consultation is\ndeliberately easier to book than a session: wider hours, and Sundays open.\n\n### Other live behaviour\n\n- Payment is taken **on booking**. The Stripe page holds the slot 30 minutes and\n  then dies unrecoverably, stranding the slot. See [[CALCOM-STUCK-BOOKINGS]].\n- Booking window was widened 2026-09-08 from 6 business days to **45 calendar\n  days**, because students could see barely a week and at most one Saturday.\n- At least one date override is live: 2026-09-19, 20:00–21:10 ET, for one student.\n\n---\n\n## Ground truth: LSAT U today\n\n### Weekly hours — already correct\n\n`booking_availability`, timezone `America/Toronto`, matches Cal.com\'s Working\nHours exactly, day for day:\n\n| Day | LSAT U | Cal.com Working Hours |\n|---|---|---|\n| Mon | 14:00–20:00 | 14:00–20:00 |\n| Tue | 13:00–19:30 | 13:00–19:30 |\n| Wed | 13:00–19:00 | 13:00–19:00 |\n| Thu | 13:00–19:30 | 13:00–19:30 |\n| Fri | 13:00–19:30 | 13:00–19:30 |\n| Sat | 14:00–17:00 | 14:00–17:00 |\n| Sun | closed | closed |\n\n### Event types\n\n| Slug | Min | Price | Buffers | Notice | Window |\n|---|---:|---:|---|---:|---|\n| `single-session` | 70 | $45.00 | 0 / 0 | 24 h | 6 business days |\n| `consultation` | 30 | free | 30 / 30 | 24 h | 5 business days |\n\n### What already works, and should not be rebuilt\n\n- Slot generation, weekly hours, buffers, per-event notice and slot pitch.\n- Google Calendar: live free/busy, event creation, Meet link, mirrored events.\n- Stripe checkout, holds, expiry sweeper, refunds, cancellation with tokens.\n- Booking confirmation and cancellation/refund emails.\n- Per-person pricing by email (`booking_price_overrides`).\n- One-off single-use links, optionally pinned to an exact time (shipped\n  2026-09-08, `booking-link` skill).\n- A hold model that is **better than Cal.com\'s**: LSAT U holds the slot, expires\n  it, and sweeps it back. The stuck-booking failure that loses Joshua sessions\n  today is a Cal.com defect that does not exist here.\n\n---\n\n## The gaps\n\nSeven items. Each says what is missing and what it costs.\n\n### 1. Per-event schedules — blocks the consultation\n\nCal.com\'s consultation runs on its own hours (7 days, 13:30–20:00). LSAT U has\nexactly one weekly-hours table, so a consultation on LSAT U would inherit\nsession hours and lose Sundays and its earlier start.\n\nNeeds: a `booking_schedules` table, `booking_availability.schedule_id`, and a\n`booking_event_types.schedule_id`, defaulting to the current rows so nothing\nmoves. Two SQL functions read availability and must take the schedule.\n\n### 2. Date overrides — a live feature with a booking already on it\n\nCal.com has at least one override open right now. LSAT U has no concept of one,\nso migrating today would silently drop it.\n\nNeeds: a `booking_date_overrides` table (date, optional window, or closed), read\nby the slot policy and the generator. A closed override must also refuse a\nbooking that is already pinned to that day.\n\n### 3. Booking window unit mismatch\n\nLSAT U counts **6 business days**; Cal.com now uses **45 calendar days**. Ported\nas-is, students would again see about a week — the exact problem Joshua fixed on\nCal.com the day before.\n\nNeeds: `window_calendar_days` alongside, or replacing, `window_business_days`.\nRecommend replacing: business days were a workaround, and calendar days are what\nthe product actually wants.\n\n### 4. Emailing a link\n\nAsked for directly. Today `booking-link` prints a URL to the terminal.\n\nNeeds: an `--email` flag that renders a short template through the existing\n`send-transactional-email` function. Everything underneath exists; this is a\ntemplate plus a flag. **The email must be a draft-and-confirm step, not a silent\nsend** — house rule.\n\n### 5. Reminders — not built\n\nCal.com sends them. LSAT U sends nothing between confirmation and the session.\nVerified: no reminder code exists anywhere in the repo.\n\nNeeds: a scheduled sweep over confirmed bookings plus a reminder template.\nNon-trivial but well-bounded, and it reuses the outbox worker pattern the single\nsession already has.\n\n### 6. Student-initiated reschedule — not built for single sessions\n\n`student-booking` has reschedule for the pass flow; the single-session path has\ncancel only. Cal.com lets an attendee reschedule from the confirmation email.\n\nNeeds: a `reschedule` action re-running the slot claim under the existing cancel\ntoken, moving the Google event rather than making a second one.\n\n### 7. Ten personal event types to move\n\nEach is a person with their own length and price. LSAT U can express this today\nas a `booking_event_types` row per student with `single_use = false` — the same\nshape Cal.com uses, minus the hand-made page. `booking_price_overrides` covers\nprice but not length, so per-person **length** is what makes a row necessary.\n\nNeeds: no new schema. A `--personal` mode on the CLI, and ten rows.\n\n---\n\n## Decisions needed from Joshua\n\nNothing below is a technical unknown; each is a product call that changes what\ngets built.\n\n1. **Booking window:** replace business days with calendar days, and set it to 45\n   to match Cal.com? *(Recommend yes.)*\n2. **Personal link slugs:** random and unguessable, or readable like\n   `/book/x/dakota`? Readable is friendlier and is what Cal.com does; random\n   means the URL alone is the credential. *(Recommend readable for standing\n   student links, random for one-offs.)*\n3. **Reminders:** wanted at cutover, or after? If wanted, at what offsets — Cal.com\'s\n   defaults are 24 h and 1 h.\n4. **Reschedule:** may a student reschedule themselves, or does that stay with\n   Joshua? Today, on LSAT U, it is Joshua\'s.\n5. **Existing future Cal.com bookings:** migrate them into LSAT U, or let them\n   run out on Cal.com and stop taking new ones there?\n6. **The consultation is the public front door.** It is the only unhidden Cal.com\n   event. Moving it changes what a stranger from the website meets, so it should\n   move last and be watched.\n\n---\n\n## Plan\n\nEach phase ends in something verifiable. No phase depends on a later one.\n\n### Phase 0 — done (2026-09-08)\n\nEvent types as data; one-off single-use links; pinned times; the 70-minute and\n$45 CHECK constraints removed. Shipped in `7ef4861`, migration applied, function\ndeployed.\n\n**Note:** this also unblocked the consultation, which could not be booked at all —\nits 30-minute insert violated a 70-minute constraint. Unverified live.\n\n### Phase 1 — parity of the rules (no new surfaces)\n\n1. Calendar-day booking window, set to 45.\n2. `booking_schedules` + `schedule_id` on availability and event types; seed\n   "Working Hours" and "Consultation" from the values in this document.\n3. `booking_date_overrides`, including the 2026-09-19 override already live.\n\n**Done when:** for each of the twelve Cal.com event types, LSAT U and Cal.com\noffer the same slots for the next 45 days. Prove it with a diff script that pulls\nCal.com `/slots` and LSAT U\'s `slots` action and reports any disagreement. That\nscript is the acceptance test for the whole migration and should be written first.\n\n### Phase 2 — the links Joshua hands out\n\n1. `--personal` mode: a standing, reusable link with its own length and price.\n2. Ten personal rows, one per current Cal.com event type.\n3. `--email` on `booking-link`: draft an email with the link, show it, send only\n   on confirmation.\n\n**Done when:** every student who has a Cal.com link has an LSAT U link that\noffers the same times at the same price, and Joshua can produce and send a new\none in a sentence.\n\n### Phase 3 — lifecycle parity\n\n1. Reminders (offsets per decision 3).\n2. Student reschedule (per decision 4).\n3. Consultation on its own schedule, verified against the live Cal.com page.\n\n**Done when:** a booking made on LSAT U produces the same sequence of emails, and\nthe same options for the student, as one made on Cal.com.\n\n### Phase 4 — cutover\n\n1. Publish the `/book/x/:slug` route (still unpublished as of this writing).\n2. Point the website, the consultation link, and every student at LSAT U.\n3. Hide every Cal.com event type; keep the account open, read-only, until the\n   last already-booked Cal.com session has happened.\n4. Run the stuck-booking sweep one last time; confirm zero future Cal.com\n   bookings; close the account.\n\n**Done when:** no future booking exists on Cal.com and no LSAT U link points at\n`cal.com`.\n\n---\n\n## What must not regress\n\n- **The hold model.** LSAT U\'s expiry-and-sweep is the fix for the Cal.com defect\n  that strands slots. Any change to the claim path keeps it.\n- **Double-booking.** Live Google free/busy is part of the slot answer, not a\n  convenience. A pinned link skips the *policy* rules; it never skips the\n  conflict check.\n- **The 6-business-day window is not the target.** Copying the current LSAT U\n  value forward would reintroduce a problem already solved.\n- **No silent sends.** Every email this work adds is drafted for Joshua and sent\n  on his confirmation.\n\n## Open questions\n\n- Are any of the ten personal event types dead? Several titles suggest former\n  students. Worth pruning before creating ten rows — check each against\n  `students/` before migrating.\n- Cal.com\'s per-event booking window was not readable through the API version\n  used here; the 45-day figure comes from the runbook, not from a live read.\n  Confirm in the dashboard before Phase 1 locks it in.\n\r\n'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 7.2 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '/**\r\n * One-off booking links: mint a URL that sells exactly one session, at a price\r\n * you set, and then stops working.\r\n *\r\n * A one-off link is not a new booking system. It is a row in\r\n * `booking_event_types` -- the same table that defines the ordinary session and\r\n * the consultation -- with a random slug and `single_use` set. Every layer\r\n * beneath it (the page, the edge function, the slot policy, the claim\r\n * transaction) is already keyed by event slug, so the link books through\r\n * exactly the code a normal session books through.\r\n *\r\n * Two knobs make it useful for short notice:\r\n *\r\n *   --at      pins one exact time. The link offers only that time, and the\r\n *             notice, window and weekly-hours rules do not apply -- you already\r\n *             decided this hour is fine by choosing it. A genuine clash with\r\n *             your calendar still blocks it, both here and at booking time.\r\n *   --notice  how far ahead someone must book, in minutes. 0 means "any time\r\n *             from now". Only meaningful without --at.\r\n *\r\n *   npx vite-node scripts/booking-link.ts -- --for "Dakota" --price 45 --at "2026-09-10 18:00"\r\n *   npx vite-node scripts/booking-link.ts -- --for "Dakota" --price 60 --notice 0 --window 3\r\n *   npx vite-node scripts/booking-link.ts -- --list\r\n *   npx vite-node scripts/booking-link.ts -- --revoke session-x7k2m9qp4rtv\r\n *\r\n * Credentials are read from ~/ai-os/.env (git-ignored), never from this repo\'s\r\n * .env, which IS tracked in git. Same convention as scripts/assign.ts.\r\n */\r\nimport fs from "node:fs";\r\nimport path from "node:path";\r\nimport { randomInt } from "node:crypto";\r\nimport { fileURLToPath } from "node:url";\r\nimport { supabase } from "@/integrations/supabase/client";\r\n\r\nconst REPO = path.resolve(path.dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url)), "..");\r\nconst APP_ORIGIN = "https://lsatprep.study";\r\n\r\nfunction readEnv(file: string): Record<string, string> {\r\n  const out: Record<string, string> = {};\r\n  if (!fs.existsSync(file)) return out;\r\n  for (const line of fs.readFileSync(file, "utf8").split(/\\r?\\n/)) {\r\n    const t = line.trim();\r\n    if (!t || t.startsWith("#")) continue;\r\n    const i = t.indexOf("=");\r\n    if (i < 0) continue;\r\n    out[t.slice(0, i).trim()] = t.slice(i + 1).trim().replace(/^["\']|["\']$/g, "");\r\n  }\r\n  return out;\r\n}\r\n\r\nconst env = {\r\n  ...readEnv(path.resolve(REPO, "../../.env")),\r\n  ...readEnv(path.join(REPO, ".env")),\r\n};\r\n\r\n// ---------------------------------------------------------------- arguments\r\n\r\nfunction arg(name: string): string | undefined {\r\n  const i = process.argv.indexOf(`--${name}`);\r\n  return i >= 0 ? process.argv[i + 1] : undefined;\r\n}\r\nconst has = (name: string) => process.argv.includes(`--${name}`);\r\n\r\nconst USAGE =\r\n  "usage: vite-node scripts/booking-link.ts -- \\\\\\n" +\r\n  "         --for <name> --price <dollars> [--at \\"YYYY-MM-DD HH:mm\\"] [--minutes 70]\\\\\\n" +\r\n  "         [--notice <minutes>] [--window <business days>] [--label <text>] [--note <text>]\\\\\\n" +\r\n  "       vite-node scripts/booking-link.ts -- --list\\\\\\n" +\r\n  "       vite-node scripts/booking-link.ts -- --revoke <slug>";\r\n\r\n// ------------------------------------------------------------------- timezone\r\n\r\n/** How far `tz` is from UTC at that instant, in milliseconds. */\r\nfunction tzOffsetMs(tz: string, date: Date): number {\r\n  const parts = new Intl.DateTimeFormat("en-US", {\r\n    timeZone: tz, hour12: false,\r\n    year: "numeric", month: "2-digit", day: "2-digit",\r\n    hour: "2-digit", minute: "2-digit", second: "2-digit",\r\n  }).formatToParts(date);\r\n  const at = Object.fromEntries(parts.map((part) => [part.type, part.value])) as Record<string, string>;\r\n  const asUtc = Date.UTC(+at.year, +at.month - 1, +at.day, +at.hour % 24, +at.minute, +at.second);\r\n  return asUtc - date.getTime();\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * A wall-clock time in Joshua\'s timezone, as a real instant. Done in two passes\r\n * because the offset itself depends on the instant: a naive single pass lands an\r\n * hour out on the two days a year the clocks move.\r\n */\r\nfunction hostWallClockToUtc(tz: string, text: string): Date | null {\r\n  const match = /^(\\d{4})-(\\d{2})-(\\d{2})[T ](\\d{1,2}):(\\d{2})$/.exec(text.trim());\r\n  if (!match) {\r\n    const iso = new Date(text);\r\n    return Number.isFinite(iso.getTime()) ? iso : null;\r\n  }\r\n  const [, y, mo, d, h, mi] = match;\r\n  const naive = Date.UTC(+y, +mo - 1, +d, +h, +mi);\r\n  const first = naive - tzOffsetMs(tz, new Date(naive));\r\n  const second = naive - tzOffsetMs(tz, new Date(first));\r\n  return new Date(second);\r\n}\r\n\r\nfunction inHostTz(tz: string, iso: string): string {\r\n  return new Date(iso).toLocaleString("en-US", {\r\n    timeZone: tz, weekday: "short", month: "short", day: "numeric",\r\n    hour: "numeric", minute: "2-digit", timeZoneName: "short",\r\n  });\r\n}\r\n\r\n// ----------------------------------------------------------------- the slug\r\n\r\n// No 0/O/1/l/i: these get read aloud and typed by hand.\r\nconst ALPHABET = "abcdefghjkmnpqrstuvwxyz23456789";\r\n\r\nfunction randomTail(length = 12): string {\r\n  let out = "";\r\n  for (let i = 0; i < length; i++) out += ALPHABET[randomInt(ALPHABET.length)];\r\n  return out;\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * Short enough to send in a text, and nothing but the secret.\r\n *\r\n * The name is deliberately not in the slug. It would lengthen a URL that gets\r\n * read aloud and retyped, and it would put a student\'s name in anything that\r\n * logs the path. Who a link is for is kept in `note`, admin-side.\r\n */\r\nfunction slugFor(): string {\r\n  return randomTail(10);\r\n}\r\n\r\n// -------------------------------------------------------------------- signin\r\n\r\nasync function signIn(): Promise<void> {\r\n  const email = env.LSATPREP_ADMIN_EMAIL;\r\n  const password = [REDACTED];\r\n  if (!email || !password) {\r\n    console.error(\r\n      "booking-link: LSATPREP_ADMIN_EMAIL / LSATPREP_ADMIN_PASSWORD are not set.\\n" +\r\n        "  Add them to ~/ai-os/.env (git-ignored). This repo\'s .env is TRACKED in git,\\n" +\r\n        "  so a credential placed there would be committed.",\r\n    );\r\n    process.exit(2);\r\n  }\r\n  const { error } = await supabase.auth.signInWithPassword({ email, password });\r\n  if (error) {\r\n    console.error(`booking-link: sign-in failed: ${error.message}`);\r\n    process.exit(1);\r\n  }\r\n}\r\n\r\nasync function hostTimezone(): Promise<string> {\r\n  const { data } = await supabase.from("booking_availability_config").select("timezone").eq("id", 1).maybeSingle();\r\n  return data?.timezone || "America/Toronto";\r\n}\r\n\r\n/**\r\n * The times this link can actually sell, asked of the booking engine itself.\r\n *\r\n * This is deliberately not a reimplementation of the availability rules. The\r\n * `slots` action unions the mirrored calendar, live Google free/busy and every\r\n * held session -- none of which is reachable from a script -- so asking it is\r\n * the only way to know a pinned time is genuinely free. An empty answer for a\r\n * pinned link means exactly one thing: that hour is already taken.\r\n */\r\nasync function slotsFor(slug: string): Promise<{ start_at: string }[]> {\r\n  const { data, error } = await supabase.functions.invoke("single-session", {\r\n    body: { action: "slots", event: slug },\r\n  });\r\n  if (error) throw new Error(error.message);\r\n  if ((data as { error?: string })?.error) throw new Error((data as { error: string }).error);\r\n  return ((data as { slots?: { start_at: string }[] }).slots) ?? [];\r\n}\r\n\r\n// --------------------------------------------------------------------- modes\r\n\r\nasync function list(tz: string): Promise<void> {\r\n  const { data, error } = await supabase\r\n    .from("booking_event_types")\r\n    .select("slug,label,length_minutes,price_cents,active,pinned_start_at,note,created_at")\r\n    .eq("single_use", true)\r\n    .order("created_at", { ascending: false });\r\n  if (error) throw error;\r\n  if (!data?.length) {\r\n    console.log("No one-off links yet.");\r\n    return;\r\n  }\r\n  for (const row of data) {\r\n    const spent = await supabase.rpc("booking_event_is_spent", { _event_slug: row.slug });\r\n    const state = !row.active ? "revoked" : spent.data === true ? "used" : "open";\r\n    console.log(\r\n      `${state.padEnd(8)} ${`$${(row.price_cents / 100).toFixed(2)}`.padEnd(9)} ${String(row.length_minutes).padStart(3)}m  ` +\r\n        `${row.pinned_start_at ? inHostTz(tz, row.pinned_start_at) : "any open time"}\\n` +\r\n        `         ${APP_ORIGIN}/book/x/${row.slug}${row.note ? `\\n         ${row.note}` : ""}\\n`,\r\n    );\r\n  }\r\n}\r\n\r\nasync function revoke(slug: string): Promise<void> {\r\n  const { data, error } = await supabase\r\n    .from("booking_event_types")\r\n    .update({ active: false, updated_at: new Date().toISOString() })\r\n    .eq("slug", slug)\r\n    .eq("single_use", true)\r\n    .select("slug")\r\n    .maybeSingle();\r\n  if (error) throw error;\r\n  if (!data) {\r\n    console.error(`booking-link: no one-off link with slug "${slug}".`);\r\n    process.exit(1);\r\n  }\r\n  console.log(`Revoked. ${APP_ORIGIN}/book/x/${slug} no longer resolves.`);\r\n}\r\n\r\nasync function create(tz: string): Promise<void> {\r\n  const forName = arg("for");\r\n  const priceText = arg("price");\r\n  if (!forName || !priceText) {\r\n    console.error(USAGE);\r\n    process.exit(2);\r\n  }\r\n\r\n  const price = Number(priceText);\r\n  if (!Number.isFinite(price) || price <= 0) {\r\n    console.error("--price must be a positive number of dollars, e.g. --price 45");\r\n    process.exit(2);\r\n  }\r\n  const priceCents = Math.round(price * 100);\r\n\r\n  const minutes = Number(arg("minutes") ?? 70);\r\n  if (!Number.isInteger(minutes) || minutes <= 0) {\r\n    console.error("--minutes must be a whole number of minutes");\r\n    process.exit(2);\r\n  }\r\n\r\n  const atText = arg("at");\r\n  let pinnedStartAt: string | null = null;\r\n  if (atText) {\r\n    const pinned = hostWallClockToUtc(tz, atText);\r\n    if (!pinned) {\r\n      console.error(`--at must be "YYYY-MM-DD HH:mm" (in ${tz}) or a full ISO timestamp, got "${atText}"`);\r\n      process.exit(2);\r\n    }\r\n    if (pinned.getTime() <= Date.now()) {\r\n      console.error(`--at is in the past: ${inHostTz(tz, pinned.toISOString())}`);\r\n      process.exit(2);\r\n    }\r\n    pinnedStartAt = pinned.toISOString();\r\n  }\r\n\r\n  // A pinned link has already answered the notice question, so the column only\r\n  // matters for a picker link. Without --at, the default stays the ordinary 24\r\n  // hours rather than silently loosening every link that omits the flag.\r\n  const notice = Number(arg("notice") ?? (pinnedStartAt ? 0 : 1440));\r\n  const window = Number(arg("window") ?? 6);\r\n  if (!Number.isInteger(notice) || notice < 0 || !Number.isInteger(window) || window <= 0) {\r\n    console.error("--notice must be >= 0 minutes and --window a positive number of business days");\r\n    process.exit(2);\r\n  }\r\n\r\n  const slug = slugFor();\r\n  const row = {\r\n    slug,\r\n    label: arg("label") ?? "LSAT Session",\r\n    length_minutes: minutes,\r\n    slot_interval_minutes: null,\r\n    minimum_notice_minutes: notice,\r\n    buffer_before_minutes: 0,\r\n    buffer_after_minutes: 0,\r\n    window_business_days: window,\r\n    price_cents: priceCents,\r\n    currency: "usd",\r\n    requires_payment: true,\r\n    active: true,\r\n    single_use: true,\r\n    pinned_start_at: pinnedStartAt,\r\n    note: arg("note") ?? `One-off link for ${forName}.`,\r\n  };\r\n\r\n  const { error } = await supabase.from("booking_event_types").insert(row);\r\n  if (error) throw error;\r\n\r\n  // Ask the booking engine what this link can sell. A pinned link that comes\r\n  // back empty clashes with something already on the calendar, and a link that\r\n  // would fail the moment it was opened is worse than no link -- so it is\r\n  // deleted rather than handed over. ## assistant

❓ **Q1** - **What “full control” includes**: Should the CLI manage only booking configuration and links, or also existing appointments, such as listing, cancelling, rescheduling, refunding, and viewing payment or calendar status?

➡️ I recommend both. It should be the command-line control center for all LSAT Prep scheduling, not merely a link editor.

---

❓ **Q2** - **How commands are organized**: Do you prefer a small number of broad commands that edit fields generically, or task-focused commands such as `events set-notice`, `availability add-window`, `links create`, and `bookings reschedule`?

➡️ I recommend task-focused commands. They are easier to discover and can validate the change before it reaches production.

---

❓ **Q3** - **How live changes are applied**: Should a mutation take effect as soon as the command runs, or should every change first show a preview and require confirmation, with a `--yes` option when you intentionally want it immediate?

➡️ I recommend preview plus confirmation by default, with `--yes` for deliberate non-interactive use. It preserves full control without turning a typo into a live scheduling mistake.

---

❓ **Q4** - **Removing things**: When retiring an event type or booking link, should the CLI only deactivate it so history remains intact, or allow permanent deletion too?

➡️ I recommend deactivate-only for normal commands. Permanent deletion should stay out of the first version.

❓ **Q5** - **How you run it**: The repo already exposes utilities as `npm run booking:link -- …`. Should this become one command, such as `npm run schedule -- …`, with a concise built-in `help` that lists every available action?

➡️ I recommend one `schedule` command, grouped by nouns: `events`, `availability`, `overrides`, `links`, and `bookings`. For example: `npm run schedule -- events list` and `npm run schedule -- events set-notice single-session 960`.

---

❓ **Q6** - **What confirmation looks like**: Before any live modification, should the CLI print a plain-English summary and the exact before/after values, then require you to type `APPLY`? Or is a simple `Continue? y/N` prompt enough?

➡️ I recommend the summary plus `APPLY`. It stays concise, makes the impending change unmistakable, and is hard to confirm accidentally.

---

❓ **Q7** - **CLI only or MCP-ready foundation**: Should version one be exclusively a terminal tool, while its internal scheduling operations are structured so an MCP can reuse them later? Or do you want an MCP in the first build as well?

➡️ I recommend CLI-only first, built around reusable scheduling operations. It gives you the control now without adding an AI-facing tool surface or duplicate authorization path.

❓ **Q8** - **MCP mutation confirmation**: MCP tools cannot use the terminal’s interactive prompt reliably. Should an MCP change follow two calls: first preview the exact change, then apply it only after you explicitly confirm in the chat? Or may an MCP tool apply a change in one call when you say to do it?

➡️ I recommend preview, then apply with a short-lived confirmation token. It keeps MCP actions aligned with the CLI’s “show me, then I confirm” rule.

---

❓ **Q9** - **Cancellations and refunds**: When cancelling an existing paid appointment, should “cancel” only cancel the booking and its calendar event, with refunds always a separate explicit `refund` action? Or should one confirmed cancellation offer to refund automatically?

➡️ I recommend separate actions. Cancellation is operational; a refund moves money. Keeping them distinct makes the record and intent unambiguous.

❓ **Q10** - **Effect on booked sessions**: If you change an event’s length, price, notice, or availability, should that affect only bookings made afterward, while existing confirmed sessions keep their original terms?

➡️ I recommend future bookings only. Existing bookings are records of an agreement and should change only through a booking-specific action.

---

❓ **Q11** - **Link types**: Should the CLI and MCP support both reusable links and one-off links from the start? Reusable links remain active until you deactivate them; one-off links close after one paid booking or can be pinned to one exact slot.

➡️ I recommend both. They cover the standing student-link and “here is your session” use cases without maintaining separate tools.

---

❓ **Q12** - **Availability controls**: Should “full control” include both recurring weekly hours and specific-date exceptions, including opening extra time or closing a date entirely?

➡️ I recommend yes. These are the two scheduling controls you would otherwise keep returning to the web admin for.

❓ **Q13** - **Where the MCP runs**: Should the MCP be a local, stdio server available only on your own computer through Codex or other approved local clients, using the same credentials as the CLI? Or should it be a remotely hosted service?

➡️ I recommend local-only. It avoids exposing scheduling controls over the internet and keeps authorization within the existing LSAT Prep admin account.

---

❓ **Q14** - **Timezone control**: Scheduling is currently based on `America/Toronto`. Should the CLI let you change the system timezone, or should it treat Toronto time as a protected setting and let you control every other scheduling rule?

➡️ I recommend protecting the timezone in version one. Changing it can silently reinterpret existing weekly hours and appointments. Every command can still display and accept Toronto time clearly.

---

❓ **Q15** - **Giving a new link to a student**: Should link commands only create and display/copy a booking URL, or should they also create a draft email or text you can review and send separately?

➡️ I recommend create and display the URL first, plus an optional draft message. Sending stays outside the scheduling tool unless you explicitly request it, consistent with your draft-before-send rule.

A remotely hosted MCP is workable, but it changes one security detail: the CLI currently signs in using an LSAT Prep admin email and password from your local `.env`. A hosted service should not receive or retain that password. It needs its own access credentials.

❓ **Q16** - **Remote MCP access**: Should the hosted MCP issue named, revocable access tokens for each approved client, stored beside the CLI credentials in `.env`? For example, you could authorize Codex on this computer, revoke it later, and issue another token for a different approved client.

➡️ I recommend named, revocable tokens. They provide the same practical access as the CLI while letting you see, rotate, or revoke individual clients without changing your LSAT Prep password.

---

❓ **Q17** - **Change history**: Should every successful scheduling mutation record who made it, when, the command or MCP tool used, and the before/after values in an immutable admin audit log?

➡️ I recommend yes. With remote write access and full control, this is how you can trace or reverse a mistaken change without relying on terminal history.

## assistant

You want one tool that lets you control all LSAT Prep scheduling from your devices.

There are two ways to run it:

1. **Local CLI:** The command runs on each computer. It uses the LSAT Prep admin login stored on that computer.

2. **Hosted tool:** The scheduling tool runs on a server. Your computer connects to it. The server needs a key to know the request is from you.

I was not suggesting other people get access. A separate key per device is only useful if you lose a laptop or want to remove access from one device. You could disable that one key without changing your LSAT Prep password everywhere.

For your goal, I recommend the hosted tool with one private access key for each of your own devices.

Do you want that setup, or do you prefer the tool to run only on each device with your existing LSAT Prep login?

## assistant

Local is the better fit here. The CLI and MCP will run on your own device, use the existing LSAT Prep admin login from its local `.env`, and never expose a scheduling service to the internet. The MCP is simply another local way for an approved assistant on that device to call the same scheduling controls.

I’ll keep an audit history, but it will identify the device and action rather than pretending there are multiple operators.

➡️ I recommend yes. You get a simple everyday interface without blocking later automation.

➡️ I recommend yes. The scheduling change can happen after confirmation; a message is separate and remains a draft unless you choose it.

I had the model wrong. There are three separate things:

- A **standard single-session link**. This is the normal LSAT U link, currently $45. A student pays for one session when they book.
- Other **single-session links**, such as a reduced-rate link. You create these when you want a different price or rule.
- A student’s **booking pass**. This is their personal link when they have already paid for a package and are owed sessions. It does not charge them again.

A booking pass must always be specific to that student. The standard single-session link does not need to be student-specific.

The ONLY skill you invoke after brainstorming is writing-plans.\n\n## The Process\n\n**Understanding the idea:**\n\n- Check out the current project state first (files, docs, recent commits)\n- Before asking detailed questions, assess scope: if the request describes multiple independent subsystems (e.g., "build a platform with chat, file storage, billing, and analytics"), flag this immediately. Don\'t spend questions refining details of a project that needs to be decomposed first.\n- If the project is too large for a single spec, help the user decompose into sub-projects: what are the independent pieces, how do they relate, what order should they be built? Then brainstorm the first sub-project through the normal design flow. Each sub-project gets its own spec → plan → implementation cycle.\n- For appropriately-scoped projects, ask questions one at a time to refine the idea\n- Prefer multiple choice questions when possible, but open-ended is fine too\n- Only one question per message - if a topic needs more exploration, break it into multiple questions\n- Focus on understanding: purpose, constraints, success criteria\n\n**Exploring approaches:**\n\n- Propose 2-3 different approaches with trade-offs\n- Present options conversationally with your recommendation and reasoning\n- Lead with your recommended option and explain why\n\n**Presenting the design:**\n\n- Once you believe you understand what you\'re building, present the design\n- Scale each section to its complexity: a few sentences if straightforward, up to 200-300 words if nuanced\n- Ask after each section whether it looks right so far\n- Cover: architecture, components, data flow, error handling, testing\n- Be ready to go back and clarify if something doesn\'t make sense\n\n**Design for isolation and clarity:**\n\n- Break the system into smaller units that each have one clear purpose, communicate through well-defined interfaces, and can be understood and tested independently\n- For each unit, you should be able to answer: what does it do, how do you use it, and what does it depend on?\n- Can someone understand what a unit does without reading its internals? Can you change the internals without breaking consumers? If not, the boundaries need work.\n- Smaller, well-bounded units are also easier for you to work with - you reason better about code you can hold in context at once, and your edits are more reliable when files are focused. When a file grows large, that\'s often a signal that it\'s doing too much.\n\n**Working in existing codebases:**\n\n- Explore the current structure before proposing changes. Follow existing patterns.\n- Where existing code has problems that affect the work (e.g., a file that\'s grown too large, unclear boundaries, tangled responsibilities), include targeted improvements as part of the design - the way a good developer improves code they\'re working in.\n- Don\'t propose unrelated refactoring. Stay focused on what serves the current goal.\n\n## After the Design\n\n**Documentation:**\n\n- Write the validated design (spec) to `docs/superpowers/specs/YYYY-MM-DD-<topic>-design.md`\n  - (User preferences for spec location override this default)\n- Use elements-of-style:writing-clearly-and-concisely skill if available\n- Commit the design document to git\n\n**Spec Self-Review:**\nAfter writing the spec document, look at it with fresh eyes:\n\n1. **Placeholder scan:** Any "TBD", "TODO", incomplete sections, or vague requirements? Does the architecture match the feature descriptions?\n3. **Scope check:** Is this focused enough for a single implementation plan, or does it need decomposition?\n4. **Ambiguity check:** Could any requirement be interpreted two different ways? No need to re-review — just fix and move on.\n\n**User Review Gate:**\nAfter the spec review loop passes, ask the user to review the written spec before proceeding:\n\n> "Spec written and committed to `<path>`. Available as a tool — not a mode. Accepting the companion means it\'s available for questions that benefit from visual treatment; it does NOT mean every question goes through the browser.\n\n**Offering the companion (just-in-time):** Do NOT offer it upfront. Wait until a question would genuinely be clearer shown than told — a real mockup / layout / diagram question, not merely a UI *topic*. The first time that happens, offer it then, as its own message:\n> "This next part might be easier if I show you — I can put together mockups, diagrams, and comparisons in a browser tab as we go. It\'s still new and can be token-intensive. Want me to? I\'ll open it for you."\n\n**This offer MUST be its own message.** Only the offer — no clarifying question, summary, or other content. Wait for the user\'s response. If they accept, start the server with `--open` so their browser opens to the first screen automatically. If they decline, continue text-only and don\'t offer again unless they raise it.\n\n**Per-question decision:** Even after the user accepts, decide FOR EACH QUESTION whether to use the browser or the terminal. The test: **would the user understand this better by seeing it than reading it?**\n\n- **Use the browser** for content that IS visual — mockups, wireframes, layout comparisons, architecture diagrams, side-by-side visual designs\n- **Use the terminal** for content that is text — requirements questions, conceptual choices, tradeoff lists, A/B/C/D text options, scope decisions\n\nA question about a UI topic is not automatically a visual question. "What does personality mean in this context?" is a conceptual question — use the terminal. "Which wizard layout works better?" is a visual question — use the browser.\n\nIf they agree to the companion, read the detailed guide before proceeding:\n`skills/brainstorming/visual-companion.md`\n\r\n'}]

## assistant

The default screen will be a concise overview, not a blank prompt. It will show every active link, its price and purpose, active booking passes, upcoming sessions, and any calendar issue before you choose an action.

There are three sensible ways to build this:

1. **Extend the existing link script.** Add more commands around `booking-link.ts`.

   Fastest at first, but it would turn one script into the control center for links, passes, bookings, pricing, calendar availability, undo, and MCP. I do not recommend it.

2. **One scheduling core with two entrances.** Build the scheduling rules and live LSAT Prep actions once. The installable `lsat-schedule` command uses them, and the local MCP uses the same operations. The command can offer a menu, direct commands, readable output, and `--json`. Every change previews, asks yes/no, writes an audit record, and can be undone when appropriate.

   This is my recommendation. It gives you one source of truth and does not let the CLI and MCP drift apart.

3. **Expand the web admin instead.** Put the controls in the LSAT Prep admin page and add a thin CLI later.

   This is useful for visual work but does not meet your goal of a command you can use from any computer.

Do you approve approach 2 as the design direction?

## user

&#x20;Do option two, then I will do option three after


## assistant

Approach 2 is the right first build. The web admin expansion becomes a later project that uses the same scheduling core, rather than a second system.

### Design: one scheduling system, two ways to use it

The LSAT Prep repository will contain a shared scheduling layer. It will know how to read and change LSAT Prep event types, links, availability, date overrides, bookings, booking passes, prices, and refunds.

Two local tools use that same layer:

- `lsat-schedule`: the installable command for Windows and Mac. Running it with no arguments shows your scheduling overview and guided menu. Direct commands remain available for fast work.
- A local MCP server: Codex or another approved tool on your computer can ask for the same overview or request the same change.

Both use the LSAT Prep admin credentials stored locally on that device. Neither connects to Cal.com or exposes a public server.

The web booking pages remain separate. Students see times in their own timezone. You see and enter times in America/Toronto.

Does this architecture look right so far?

## user

&#x20;Okay. Yeah, it looks good. I want to have the ability to do it on the website, but we'll do that later, or take that approach. And yes, the architecture looks good as you presented it. Now, let's create it.


## assistant

I’m holding implementation for one more design section, because “full control” depends on the exact change flow. Once you approve it, I’ll write the design spec and move to the implementation plan.

### Design: how a change works

`lsat-schedule` starts with an overview:

- Active standard and custom links, including price, duration, paid or free, and status
- Booking passes and owed sessions
- Upcoming bookings
- Calendar connection status and conflicts

You can use the menu or a direct command. For example: change the price of the standard single-session link, create a free reusable link, create a one-time link, add owed sessions to a booking pass, or reschedule a booking.

Before a live change, the tool says exactly what will change and asks `Continue? y/N`. A “yes” makes the change. Existing bookings keep their original price and terms. Changes apply only to future bookings.

Each completed change saves a history entry with its before and after values. `undo` can restore configuration, link, availability, and booking-pass changes. It cannot reverse a refund, a completed payment, or a sent message.

For a cancellation or reschedule, the tool asks whether to notify the student. If you say yes, it creates a draft. Otherwise it sends nothing. Link creation only prepares a draft when you explicitly ask for one.

Does this change flow look right?

## user

&#x20;Create this. Go, create it.


## assistant

I have approval for the architecture and change flow. I need to lock the data-integrity and verification rules before code begins, so the CLI cannot bypass the booking safeguards that already protect live sessions.

## assistant

### Design: live data and safeguards

The tool reads and updates the same LSAT Prep scheduling records the booking pages use. It does not create a second calendar or a second source of truth.

It will preserve the current protections:

- Google Calendar conflicts still block a slot.
- A paid booking still uses the existing Stripe hold and expiry flow.
- A booking pass remains the only record of sessions owed.
- Deactivating a link preserves its history and stops future use.
- Free links skip payment but still check availability.
- A custom link can be reusable, single-use, or pinned to one exact time.
- The standard link keeps a stable URL; its price and future-booking rules can change.
- Students see booking times in their local timezone; all operator input and CLI output use America/Toronto.

### Design: verification

Before releasing it, we will test the shared scheduling layer independently, then test both the CLI and MCP against it. We will cover price changes, free and paid links, one-time links, booking-pass changes, cancellation, rescheduling, refunds, notifications remaining drafts, audit history, undo, calendar conflicts, and timezone display.

This is the final design section. Approve it, and I’ll write the spec and move directly to the implementation plan.

## user

&#x20;Approve it. I approve, go.


## assistant

Design approved. I’ll document the agreed LSAT Prep-only CLI and local MCP design in the LSAT Prep repository, review it for gaps, then create the implementation plan.

It keeps the current\n+Google Calendar configuration, reports its connection health, and respects its\n+free/busy conflicts.
